I.  INTRODUCTION AND QUALIFICATIONS  
1 
Q. Please state your name and business address. 
2 
A. My name is Kimbugwe A. Kateregga.  My business address is 17595 S. Tamiami Trail, 
3 
Suite 260, Fort Myers, Florida 33908. 
4 
 
5 
Q. By whom are you employed and in what capacity? 
6 
A. I am employed as a Vice President of Foster Associates Consultants, LLC (“Foster 
7 
Associates”).  Foster Associates is a public utility economic consulting firm offering 
8 
economic research and consulting services on issues and problems arising from gov-
9 
ernmental regulation of business.  Areas of specialization supported by the firm’s Fort 
10 
Myers office include property service–life forecasting, technological forecasting, de-
11 
preciation estimation, and valuation of industrial property. 
12 
 
13 
Q. Please describe your educational training and professional background. 
14 
A. I was awarded a B.Sc. (Eng.) degree in Civil Engineering from Makerere University, a 
15 
M.Sc. degree in Management Science from the University of Wales, and a Ph.D. degree 
16 
in Engineering Valuation from Iowa State University.  I have taught college level 
17 
courses in statistics and mathematics and conducted courses in depreciation and public 
18 
utility economics for clients of Foster Associates.  
19 
 
20 
I have prepared and presented a number of papers at conferences and have published 
21 
several articles on matters relating to depreciation, technological forecasting, valuation,

II. PURPOSE OF TESTIMONY

A. Foster Associates was engaged by Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National

3

9 
and Gas Rate Plans in Accordance with Joint Proposal” (issued March 15, 2013) in 
10 
Case 12−G−0202.  The purpose of my testimony is to sponsor and describe the 2019 
11 
gas depreciation study conducted by Foster Associates.

IV. DEVELOPMENT OF DEPRECIATION RATES  
21 


--- Page 6 ---
Direct Testimony of Dr. Kimbugwe A. Kateregga 
 
 
Page 4 of 16 
 
Q. Please explain why depreciation studies are needed for accounting and ratemak-
1 
ing purposes. 
2 
A. The goal of depreciation accounting is to charge to operations a reasonable estimate of 
3 
the cost of the service potential of an asset (or group of assets) consumed during an 
4 
accounting interval.  A number of depreciation systems have been developed to achieve 
5 
this objective, most of which employ time as the apportionment base. 
6 
 
7 
Implementation of a time–based (or age–life) system of depreciation accounting re-
8 
quires the estimation of several parameters or statistics related to a plant account.  The 
9 
average service life of a vintage, for example, is a statistic that will not be known with 
10 
certainty until all units from the original placement have been retired from service.  A 
11 
vintage average service life, therefore, must be estimated initially and periodically re-
12 
vised as indications of the eventual average service life become more certain.  Future 
13 
net salvage rates and projection curves, which describe the expected distribution of 
14 
retirements over time, are also estimated parameters of a depreciation system that are 
15 
subject to future revisions.  Depreciation studies should be conducted periodically to 
16 
assess the continuing reasonableness of parameters and accrual rates derived from prior 
17 
estimates. 
18 
 
19 
The need for periodic depreciation studies is also a derivative of the ratemaking pro-
20 
cess, which establishes prices for utility services based on costs.  For a regulated utility, 
21 
recovery of investor–supplied capital is dependent upon allowed revenues, which are 
22 
in turn dependent upon approved levels of depreciation expense.  Periodic reviews of 
23 


--- Page 7 ---
Direct Testimony of Dr. Kimbugwe A. Kateregga 
 
 
Page 5 of 16 
 
depreciation rates are, therefore, essential to the achievement of timely capital recovery 
1 
for a regulated utility. 
2 
 
3 
It is also important to recognize that revenue associated with depreciation is a signifi-
4 
cant source of internally generated funds used to finance plant replacements and new 
5 
capacity additions.  This is not to suggest that internal cash generation should be sub-
6 
stituted for the goals of depreciation accounting.  Internally generated funds, however, 
7 
incur no incremental financing costs and the potential for realizing a reduction in the 
8 
marginal cost of external financing provides an added incentive for conducting periodic 
9 
depreciation studies and adopting proper depreciation rates. 
10 
 
11 
Q. Please describe the principal steps involved in conducting a depreciation study. 
12 
A. The first step in conducting a depreciation study is the collection of plant accounting 
13 
data needed to conduct a statistical analysis of past retirement experience.  Data are 
14 
also collected to permit an analysis of the relationship between retirements and realized 
15 
gross salvage and cost of removal.  The data collection phase should include a verifi-
16 
cation of the accuracy of the plant accounting records and a reconciliation of the as-
17 
sembled data to the official plant records of the company. 
18 
 
19 
The next step in a depreciation study is the estimation of service life statistics from an 
20 
analysis of past retirement experience.  The term life analysis is used to describe the 
21 
activities undertaken in this step to obtain a mathematical description of the forces of 
22 


--- Page 8 ---
Direct Testimony of Dr. Kimbugwe A. Kateregga 
 
 
Page 6 of 16 
 
retirement acting upon a plant category.  The mathematical expressions used to describe 
1 
these forces are known as survival functions or survivor curves. 
2 
 
3 
Life indications obtained from an analysis of past retirement experience are blended 
4 
with expectations about the future to obtain an appropriate projection life curve.  This 
5 
step, called life estimation, is concerned with predicting the expected remaining life of 
6 
property units still exposed to the forces of retirement.  The amount of weight given to 
7 
the analysis of historical data will depend upon the extent to which past retirement 
8 
experience is considered descriptive of the future. 
9 
 
10 
An estimate of the net salvage rate applicable to future retirements is most often ob-
11 
tained from an analysis of gross salvage and cost of removal realized in the past.  An 
12 
analysis of past experience (including an examination of trends over time) provides a 
13 
baseline for estimating future salvage and cost of removal.  Consideration, however, 
14 
should be given to events that may cause deviations from net salvage observed in the 
15 
past.  Among the factors that should be considered are the age of plant retirements, the 
16 
portion of retirements that will be reused, changes in the method of removing plant, the 
17 
type of plant to be retired in the future, inflation expectations, the shape of the projec-
18 
tion life curve, and economic conditions that may warrant greater or lesser weight to 
19 
be given to the net salvage observed in the past. 
20 
 
21 
A comprehensive depreciation study will also include an analysis of the adequacy of 
22 
the recorded depreciation reserve.  The purpose of such an analysis is to compare the 
23 


--- Page 9 ---
Direct Testimony of Dr. Kimbugwe A. Kateregga 
 
 
Page 7 of 16 
 
current balance in the recorded reserve with the balance required to achieve the goals 
1 
and objectives of depreciation accounting if the amount and timing of future retire-
2 
ments and net salvage are realized exactly as predicted.  The difference between the 
3 
required (or theoretical) reserve and the recorded reserve provides a measurement of 
4 
the expected excess or shortfall that will remain in the depreciation reserve if corrective 
5 
action is not taken to extinguish the reserve imbalance. 
6 
 
7 
Although reserve records are typically maintained by various account classifications, 
8 
the total reserve for a company is the most important indicator of the adequacy (or 
9 
inadequacy) of recorded depreciation reserves.  Differences between theoretical rec-
10 
orded reserves will arise as a normal occurrence when service lives, dispersion patterns 
11 
and net salvage estimates are adjusted in the course of depreciation reviews.  Differ-
12 
ences will also arise due to plant accounting activity such as transfers and adjustments 
13 
requiring an identification of reserves at a different level from that maintained in the 
14 
accounting system.  It is appropriate, therefore, and consistent with group depreciation 
15 
theory, to redistribute recorded reserves periodically among primary accounts based on 
16 
the most recent estimate of service lives, retirement dispersion and net salvage rates.  
17 
A redistribution of the recorded reserve will provide an initial reserve balance for each 
18 
primary account consistent with the estimates of retirement dispersion selected to de-
19 
scribe mortality characteristics of the accounts and establish a baseline against which 
20 
future comparisons can be made. 
21 
 
22 
Finally, parameters estimated from service life and net salvage studies are integrated 
23 


--- Page 10 ---
Direct Testimony of Dr. Kimbugwe A. Kateregga 
 
 
Page 8 of 16 
 
into an appropriate formulation of an accrual rate based upon a selected depreciation 
1 
system.  Three elements are needed to describe a depreciation system.  The sub–ele-
2 
ments most widely used in constructing a depreciation system are shown in Figure 1 
3 
below. 
4 
The above elements (i.e., method, procedure and technique) can be visualized as three 
5 
dimensions of a cube in which each face describes a variety of sub–elements that can 
6 
be combined to form a system.  A depreciation system is therefore formed by selecting 
7 
a sub–element from each face such that the system contains one method, one procedure 
8 
and one technique. 
9 
 
10 
V. 2019 DEPRECIATION RATE STUDY 
11

12

14 
and appending annual plant, net salvage, and depreciation reserve transactions to the 
15 
database used in conducting the 2011 study.  The accuracy and completeness of the 
16 
assembled database was verified for activity years 2011 through 2018 by comparing

the beginning plant balance, additions, retirements, transfers and adjustments, and the 
1 
ending plant balance derived for each activity year to the adjusted plant records of the 
2

3

4 
had been verified in the 2011 study.

6 
Q. Did Foster Associates conduct statistical life studies for Niagara Mohawk’s gas 
7 
plant and equipment? 
8 
A. Yes. As discussed in Exhibit __ (KAK−2), all plant accounts were analyzed using a 
9 
technique in which first, second, and third degree polynomials were fitted to a set of 
10 
observed retirement ratios.  The resulting function was expressed as a survivorship 
11 
function, which was numerically integrated to obtain an estimate of the average service 
12 
life.  The observed proportions surviving were then fitted by a weighted least–squares 
13 
procedure to both the Iowa–type and h–curve families (using projection lives derived 
14 
from the graduation of hazard rates) to obtain a mathematical description or classifica-
15 
tion of the dispersion characteristics of the data.1  Service life indications derived from 
16 
the statistical analyses were blended with informed judgment and expectations about 
17 
the future to obtain an appropriate projection life and curve for each plant category.  
18 
Foster Associates obtained an understanding and assessment of expectations and vari-
19 
ations in future forces of retirement through a site and plant inspection visit and 
20 
                                                                        
1 In Case 10−E−0050, the Commission found that “... there is no well-established precedent …  
dictating the use of H-curves or to disregard Iowa curves … we stand ready to determine which 
curve provides the best fit, be it an H-curve or an Iowa curve.” (Case 10−E−0050 Rate Order 
at 62). 


--- Page 12 ---
Direct Testimony of Dr. Kimbugwe A. Kateregga 
 
 
Page 10 of 16 
 
discussions with personnel from various departments of the Company including ac-
1 
counting, planning, engineering, and regulatory. 
2 
 
3 
Q. Did Foster Associates conduct a net salvage analysis for Niagara Mohawk gas 
4 
plant and equipment? 
5 
A. Yes.  A five–year moving average analysis of the ratio of realized salvage and removal 
6 
expense to the associated retirements was used by Foster Associates to: (i) estimate a 
7 
realized net salvage rate; (ii) detect the emergence of historical trends; and (iii) establish 
8 
a basis for estimating a future net salvage rate.  Cost of removal and salvage opinions 
9 
obtained from Company personnel were blended with judgment and historical net sal-
10 
vage indications in developing estimates of the future. 
11 
 
12 
Although average net salvage rates should be estimated using direct−dollar weighting 
13 
of historical retirements with the historical net salvage rate, and future retirements (i.e., 
14 
surviving plant) with the estimated future net salvage rate, the Commission does not 
15 
distinguish between realized, average and future net salvage.  Accordingly, the current 
16 
study retains the Commission adopted use of future net salvage rates as average net 
17 
salvage rates.  
18 
 
19 
Q. Did Foster Associates conduct an analysis of recorded depreciation reserves? 
20 
A. Yes.  Statement C of Exhibit __ (KAK−2) provides a comparison of recorded computed 
21 
and redistributed reserves at December 31, 2018.  The recorded reserve for gas plant 
22

and equipment was $897,790,360 or 36.26 percent of the depreciable plant investment.  
1 
The corresponding computed reserve is $1,002,669,151 or 40.50 percent of the depre-
2 
ciable plant investment.  A reserve deficiency of $104,878,791 (or 11.68 percent of 
3 
recorded reserves) is therefore indicated for gas plant assets.   
4 
 
5

for Niagara Mohawk.

9

Q. Is Foster Associates recommending a change in the depreciation system for the 
14 
Company? 
15 
A. No.  Although Foster Associates is of the opinion that a system composed of the 
16 
straight–line method, vintage group procedure, remaining–life technique would better 
17 
achieve the goals and objectives of depreciation accounting, the Commission has his-
18 
torically prescribed the straight–line method, broad group procedure and whole–life 
19 
technique.  Accordingly, depreciation rates developed in the 2019 study for all depre-
20 
ciable categories were derived using the currently prescribed system. 
21

Q. Are you generally familiar with Niagara Mohawk’s initiative for a proactive and 
1 
accelerated leak prone pipe replacement program? 
2 
A. Yes.  It is my understanding that Niagara Mohawk is currently retiring and replacing 
3 
approximately 50 miles of leak prone pipe (“LPP”) per year in the distribution mains 
4 
and services accounts. The program, launched in 2014, is intended to improve safety 
5 
and reliability.  Foster Associates was informed that although the identified LPP pipe 
6 
investment constitutes only a small percentage of the distribution mains and services 
7 
assets, LPP accounts for about 90 percent of leak repairs annually.  The retirement of 
8 
LPP assets is supported by the Company's Distribution Integrity Management Plant, 
9 
which requires the Company to:  (i) know its distribution piping system, (ii) understand 
10 
the threats to the system, and (iii) evaluate the risks and prepare replacement programs 
11 
for its leak prone mains and service inventory to help mitigate those risks. 
12 
 
13 
Q. Did Foster Associates obtain from the Company data necessary to address LPP 
14 
in this study? 
15 
A. Yes.  The Company identified and provided to Foster Associates LPP dollars and vin-
16 
tages of investment and recorded reserves in the four affected accounts (376.11 – Mains 
17 
– Steel, 376.13 – Mains – Cast Iron, 380.10 – Service – Metallic, and 380.20 – Service 
18 
Plastic).  For the purposes of the depreciation study, the investment and recorded re-
19 
serves were then provisionally transferred out of the parent accounts and assigned to 
20 
tentative LPP subaccounts (376.31 – Mains – Steel, 376.33 – Mains – Cast Iron, 380.30 
21 
– Service – Metallic, and 380.40 – Service Plastic).  Foster Associates was additionally 
22

provided realized net salvage recorded since 2014 against LPP retirements identified 
1 
in each parent account.  The realized net salvage over that period permitted Foster As-
2

3 
LPP subaccounts.  Foster Associates was advised that the program will be completed 
4 
within nine years from the effective date of implementation of the depreciation rates 
5 
approved in this proceeding.

Q. What is the treatment of LPP presented in this study? 
8 
A. At the request of the Company, the net plant (adjusted for net salvage) in the LPP sub-
9 
accounts will be charged to operations over a nine–year period, which is equivalent to 
10 
the remaining term of the LPP replacement program.  To mitigate the bill impacts of 
11 
its rate filing in light of the COVID-19 pandemic, the Company proposes to begin this 
12 
amortization in Data Year 1, the twelve months beginning July 1, 2022.  For each LPP 
13 
subaccount, the adjusted net plant is given by:

Q. Please summarize the depreciation rates and accruals Foster Associates is rec-
1 
ommending for Niagara Mohawk gas assets in the 2019 study. 
2 
A. Table 1 below provides a summary of the changes in annual rates and accruals resulting 
3 
from an application of the proposed parameters and depreciation system currently pre-
4 
scribed for the Niagara Mohawk gas operations.  Account by account changes are pro-
5 
vided in Statements A and B of Exhibit __ (KAK−2). 
6 
Foster Associates is recommending primary account depreciation rates equivalent to a 
7 
composite rate of 2.77 percent.  Depreciation expense is currently accrued at an equiv-
8 
alent composite rate of 2.09 percent.  The recommended change in the composite de-
9 
preciation rate is, therefore, an increase of 0.68 percentage points.  
10 
 
11 
A continued application of current rates would provide annualized depreciation ex-
12 
pense of $51,785,805 compared with an annualized expense of $60,208,050 using the 
13 
proposed changes in rates due to adjustments in service life and net salvage statistics, 
14 
an increase in annualized expense of $8,422,245.  The recommended treatment of LPP 
15 
investment would add $8,270,425 to this total.  Thus in total, the combination of the 
16 
changes in depreciation rates attributable to the adjustments in service life and net

salvage statistics recommended in the 2019 study and the recommended treatment of 
1 
LPP investment would increase 2019 depreciation expense by $16,692,670.  As dis-
2 
cussed previously, the recommended treatment of LPP investment would not com-
3 
mence until Data Year 1 under the Company’s proposal.   
4 
 
5 
VI. DEPRECIATION RESERVE DEFICIENCY 
6 
Q. Do the rates shown in Table 1 address the $104,878,791 reserve deficiency esti-
7 
mated in this study? 
8 
A. No. Although Foster Associates would recommend use of remaining–life rates to ad-
9 
dress the reserve deficiency, the Commission has historically adopted whole–life rates. 
10 
 
11 
Q. How does the remaining–life technique differ from the whole–life technique? 
12 
A. The distinction between a whole–life rate and a remaining–life rate is the treatment of 
13 
depreciation reserve imbalances.  The measurement of a reserve imbalance is the dif-
14 
ference between a theoretical or computed reserve and the corresponding recorded re-
15 
serve for a rate category.  The remaining–life technique provides a systematic 
16 
amortization of these differences over the composite weighted average remaining life 
17 
of a rate category.  The whole–life technique does not address reserve imbalances.  The 
18 
formulation of a remaining–life accrual rate is given by: 
19 
𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴 𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅= 1 −𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅 𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅 𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅−𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹𝐹 𝑁𝑁𝑁𝑁𝑁𝑁 𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆 𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅
𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅 𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿
 
20 
A remaining–life accrual rate is equivalent to the sum of a whole–life rate and an amor-
21 
tization of any reserve imbalance over the estimated remaining life of a rate category. 
22 


--- Page 18 ---
Direct Testimony of Dr. Kimbugwe A. Kateregga 
 
 
Page 16 of 16 
 
Stated as an equation, a remaining–life accrual rate is equivalent to: 
1 
𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴 𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅= 1 −𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴 𝑁𝑁𝑁𝑁𝑁𝑁 𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆 𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅
𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴𝐴 𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿
2 
+ 𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶 𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅 𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅−𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅 𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅 𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟𝑟
𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑛𝑛𝑔𝑔 𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿𝐿
 
3 
As an alternative to a remaining–life accrual rate, amortization of the reserve imbalance 
4 
can be achieved by substituting an amortization period other than remaining life in the 
5 
denominator of this equation. 
6 
 
7 
Q. Is the Company proposing to amortize the $104,878,791 reserve deficiency? 
8 
A. Yes.  The Company is requesting that the Commission approve amortization of the 
9 
reserve deficiency over a ten–year period.  However, to further mitigate bill impacts 
10 
during the current COVID-19 pandemic the Company is not proposing to commence 
11 
this amortization until Data Year 2, the twelve months beginning July 1, 2023.  Foster 
12 
Associates has been advised that the Company is requesting the amortization to put the 
13 
Company in a more optimal position to address the recovery of the costs of its gas 
14 
operations as New York State continues its efforts to address climate change.  These 
15 
concerns will be discussed by the Company’s Policy Panel.  Approval of the reserve 
16 
deficiency amortization would contribute an additional $10,487,879 to the annualized 
17 
accrual shown in Table 1 above and in Statement B of Exhibit __ (KAK−2). 
18 
 
19 
Q. Does this conclude your direct testimony? 
20 
A. Yes, it does.
21

Service Life and Net Salvage Analysis and Estimation

conclusions could not, therefore, be drawn from the statistical service life analysis. Lacking any

NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Transmission Plant 
Account: 367.00  Mains 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 1 of 3 
 
Description 
This account includes the installed cost of transmission system mains. Records supporting this 
account are kept in such a way as to show separately the cost of mains of different sizes and 
types. The majority of the investment in this account is coated, wrapped, and cathodically 
protected steel pipe. Account statistics and current and proposed parameters are shown in Table 
1 below. 
 
Current
Proposed
PLife-Curve
85-R3
85-R3
Future Net Sal. Rate
-10.0%
-40.0%

-65.0%

$175,484,606

$3,466,355
Percent Retired 
2.0%
Plant Balance
$172,018,251
Table 1. Account Parameters and Statistics
 
Life Analysis and Estimation 
The Company operates approximately 568 miles of pipelines. Seven of the pipelines, constituting 
124 of the 568 miles, have been modified with pig launchers and receivers to allow the passage 
of an inline inspection (ILI) device. The Company is continuing to enhance ILI capabilities in the 
system with the goal of making all pipelines ILI enabled as technology permits. 
Physical forces of retirement acting upon transmission mains include internal and external 
corrosion, pipe fatigue, and bacterial decay. Capacity driven system upgrades also influence the 
observed service life. The renewed Pipeline Safety Act of 2011 mandated that the Pipeline and 
Hazardous Materials Safety Administration (PHMSA) establish rules requiring operators to 
demonstrate that their pipelines are "Fit For Service". The Company plans to retire and replace a 
number of pipeline sections for which adequate material and pressure test documentation is 
unavailable. It is estimated that as much as 30 to 35 miles of such pipe will be retired within the 
next 15 years and that half of that amount is likely to be retired within the next seven years. The 
Company also plans to replace Pipeline 34 ("PL 34"), 15,000 feet of eight-inch pipe, which has 
been identified as high risk because it was lap-welded. Welds produced by lap-welding are 
unreliable and manufacturers no longer use them because of integrity concerns. 
Only $3.5M of investment has been retired from this account since 1996, the earliest year in the 
observation band. The average plant balance over that period was more than $147M. The derived 
retirement rates on which the statistical service life analysis is based are insubstantial and tend to 
generate negative estimators of the hazard function. Broader observation bands are highly 
censored and do not provided reliable service life estimates. Reasonable conclusions could not, 
therefore, be drawn from the statistical service life analysis. Moreover, it is not clear to what 
extent the new regulations and Company initiatives above will affect the average service life of 
Exhibit___(KAK-3)
Page 24 of 441


--- Page 94 ---
  
  
NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Transmission Plant 
Account: 367.00  Mains 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 2 of 3 
 
this account. Without any evidence to the contrary, Foster Associates is, therefore recommending 
a retention of the currently prescribed service life parameters. 
Net Salvage Analysis 
The adjusted net salvage analysis for this account shows an overall realized net salvage rate of -
65.0 percent. The five–year rolling band ending in 2018, in which almost 95 percent of the 
historical retirements were recorded, shows a realized net salvage rate of -62.0 percent. 
Net Salvage Estimation 
No gross salvage is expected from retired pipe. Cost associated with the retirement or removal of 
pipe, however, can be substantial. Although the nature of the work needed to retire or remove 
pipe is situational, it typically involves a number of costly activities such as conditioning the site 
(i.e. obtaining permits, setting up signs and checking for the presence of other shared utilities, a 
process called "Dig Safe"), stopping the flow of gas and purging the pipe of all remaining gas, 
checking for contaminants, digging, excavating, cutting, capping, backfilling with grout, 
abandoning in place or removing pipe for safe and environmentally acceptable disposal since 
used pipe is always considered contaminated. Site restoration is invariably mandatory. 
Equipment such as backhoes, excavators, dump trucks and "suck–down trucks" which extract the 
remaining gas out of the line and into adjacent lines are almost invariably required at the site. 
Coordination with other operations personnel is often required to adjust station pressures, operate 
valves, redirect the flow of gas in order to reduce pressures and vent as little gas as possible to 
the atmosphere (for environmental reasons). All removed pipe requires testing for PCBs positive 
results dictate specific abandonment procedures. The cost incurred in retiring, replacing, or 
abandoning a main is, therefore, labor intensive, sensitive to inflation, and substantial. 
Some retirement and removal jobs will activate Article VII which is a New York State Code to 
demonstrate the need for a particular project and additionally provide the state a thorough study 
of need and the environmental impact of the project. Article VII is mandated depending on the 
size of the project and oftentimes takes years to complete the requisite documentation and obtain 
approval. 
The need to physically remove pipe is dictated by a number of conditions. Pipe removal is 
dictated when it is in the way of new construction, when issuance of a permit requires it, or in the 
case of pipe running into or through a regulator station. Pipe retired in place near or in the 
ground of a regulator station can affect corrosion prevention and also prove problematic when 
congestion of retired pipe and equipment prevents proper identification of assets. All pipe retired 
in place must be tested for liquids and actions taken if any are found. Actions could include 
directing a foam pig through the pipe to absorb and push out the liquids in a controlled manner. 
If PCBs are found during wipe sample testing, affected sections may need to be grouted or 
excavated and removed. Pipe passing under road ways, rail road tracks, or water bodies requires 
removal to prevent damage if the pipe settled or corroded over time and could no longer support 
the sustained loading. 
Exhibit___(KAK-3)
Page 25 of 441


--- Page 95 ---
  
  
NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Transmission Plant 
Account: 367.00  Mains 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 3 of 3 
 
One of the factors dictating physical removal of mains is the availability of rights of way. With 
increasing real estate development and highway construction, rights of way availability is 
expected to continue declining in the future.  
The development of a net salvage rate for transmission mains should also recognize that removal 
expense incurred in any given year is largely independent of the age of the plant retired from 
service. Moreover, because future removal expense is sensitive to inflation, it is necessary to 
consider the timing of future retirements and the age of the plant when it will most likely be 
retired from service. 
Both the historical realized net salvage and the discussion above support the expectation that 
substantial cost of retiring and removal will be incurred in the future. Based on these 
observations, Foster Associates is recommending a -40 percent future net salvage rate for this 
account to move the expectation from the currently approved -10 percent and in the direction of 
the observed historical experience.

NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Transmission Plant 
Account: 369.15  Regulating Station Equipment 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 1 of 2 
 
Description 
This account includes the cost of meters, gauges, odorants, piping, valves and other equipment 
used in measuring or regulating the flow of gas in connection with gas transmission operations. 
Account statistics and current and proposed parameters are shown in Table 1 below. 
 
Current
Proposed
PLife-Curve
40-R0.5
40-R0.5
Future Net Sal. Rate
-10.0%

$25,918,696

$24,115,162
Table 1. Account Parameters and Statistics
 
Service Life Analysis 
The retirement rates underlying the statistical service life analysis for this account are erratic and 
tend to generate negative estimators of the hazard function. Broader observation bands are highly 
censored and do not provided reliable service life estimates. 
Life Estimation 
Company operations personnel report that a number of heaters classified in this account have an 
antiquated burner management system (BMS) that requires replacement. Ten such units have 
already been replaced. They also report that to reduce carbon emissions, the Company has started 
retiring natural gas generators for odorant pumps and replacing them with nitrogen generators. 
The Company continues to review the chemistry of glycol heaters to reasonably determine the 
internal condition of the heaters. The Company expects also to retire and replace or rebuild a 
number of undersized district regulators in its "Gas System Reinforcement Program". 
It is not known, however, to what extent the combination of these initiatives will affect the 
expected average service life of this account. Without reliable indication from the statistical 
service life analysis and lacking evidence to support a revision, Foster Associates is, therefore, 
recommending a retention of the currently prescribed 40–R0.5 projection life–curve. 
Net Salvage Analysis 
The adjusted net salvage analysis conducted for this accounts shows a -7.5 percent rate over the 
study period which reasonably supports the currently prescribed -10 percent future net salvage 
rate. 
Net Salvage Estimation 
Although viable regulators, valves, chart boxes, and system automation devices can be reused, 
the majority of retired equipment is considered contaminated and disposed of according to 
regulatory procedures. Cost of removal, however, is generated from a number of events such as 
Exhibit___(KAK-3)
Page 42 of 441


--- Page 112 ---
  
  
NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Transmission Plant 
Account: 369.15  Regulating Station Equipment 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 2 of 2 
 
conditioning the site for the start of a job (e.g. setting up signs, mobilizing, and "Dig Safe" 
checking), digging and excavating, welding, cutting and capping, rental costs associated with 
equipment such as backhoes, excavators, and dump trucks, stopping gas flow, and purging the 
gas to clear lines. Additional retiring and removal costs are due to cutting and removing the old 
assets, backfilling holes, and site restoration. Based on the net salvage analysis and these 
considerations, a retention of the currently prescribed -10 percent future net salvage rate is 
recommended for this account.

NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Transmission Plant 
Account: 369.25  Regulating Station Manholes 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 1 of 1 
 
Description 
This account includes the cost of manholes and station vaults used in transmission measuring or 
regulating stations. Account statistics and current and proposed parameters are shown in Table 1 
below. 
 
Current
Proposed

Table 1. Account Parameters and Statistics
 
Service Life and Net Salvage Analysis and Estimation 
No retirements have been recorded in this account since 1996, the earliest year in the observation 
band. The 45–L0 projection life curve and -30.0 percent future net salvage rate parameters 
recommended for this account in the 2011 study were based on parameters recommended for 
Distribution Plant Account 378.20 – Measuring and Regulating Station Equipment – Manholes 
which had experienced more retirement activity and was considered comparable in service life 
and net salvage parameters to this account. Since it is reasonable to continue the comparability 
between the two accounts, Foster Associates is recommending for this account the 45–L1 
projection life–curve and -40 percent future net salvage rate parameters recommended for 
Account 378.20.

369.55 Meas. And Reg. Station Equipment - RTU 
 
 
 
369.55 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Exhibit___(KAK-3)
Page 62 of 441


--- Page 132 ---
  
  
NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Transmission Plant 
Account: 369.55  Meas. and Reg. Station Equip. - RTU 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 1 of 2 
 
Description 
This account includes the cost of remote terminal units (RTUs) used to provide remote control 
and information acquisition such as temperature, pressure, and flow data between transmission 
gas regulating substations and manned Control Centers. Where required, RTUs can also monitor 
gas detectors and intrusion alarms and allow Control Centers to adjust flow and pressure set 
points at regulator stations. Data is transmitted via phone lines or cellular networks. Account 
statistics and current and proposed parameters are shown in Table 1 below. 
 
Current
Proposed
PLife-Curve
25-H4
25-H4
Future Net Sal. Rate
-5.0%
-5.0%
Realized Net Sal. Rate
-5.6%
Average Age (yrs.)
11.2
Derived Additions
$1,727,294
Plant Retirements
$86,292
Percent Retired 
5.0%
Plant Balance
$1,641,002
Table 1. Account Parameters and Statistics
 
Service Life Analysis 
Only $86,293 of investment has been retired from this account since 1996, the earliest year in the 
observation band. The average plant balance over that period was more than $960,000. The 
retirement rates underlying the statistical service life analysis are erratic and tend to generate 
negative estimators of the underlying hazard function. Broader observation bands are highly 
censored and do not provided reliable service life estimates.  
Life Estimation 
Some of the equipment in this account such as modems and telemetry was installed many years 
ago and has become obsolete or antiquated. About 44 percent of the Company's system, for 
example, continues to rely on paper chart recorders instead of electronic system automation. The 
Company, through its "System Automation & Control" program, intends to update the equipment 
to support the standardization of telemetry across the Company's gas transmission and 
distribution systems. 
The 25–H4 projection life curve and  -5.0 percent future net salvage rate parameters 
recommended for this account in the 2011 study were based on parameters recommended for  
Distribution Plant account 378.55 – Measuring and Regulating Station Equipment – RTU which 
has experienced more retirement activity and is comparable in service life and net salvage 
parameters to this account. 
Considering these observations and continuing to rely on the parameters for Account 378.55, 
Foster Associates is recommending retention of the currently prescribed service life parameters. 
 
Exhibit___(KAK-3)
Page 63 of 441


--- Page 133 ---
  
  
NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Transmission Plant 
Account: 369.55  Meas. and Reg. Station Equip. - RTU 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 2 of 2 
 
Net Salvage Analysis 
The adjusted net salvage analysis for this account shows a -5.6 percent realized net salvage rate. 
It is, however, derived from a minimal amount of retirements.  
Net Salvage Estimation 
Without a reliable historical net salvage analysis and lacking any evidence to the contrary, Foster 
Associates is recommending a retention of the -5 percent currently prescribed future net salvage 
rate. This recommendation is also congruent to the net salvage parameters recommended for 
Distribution Plant account 378.55 – Measuring and Regulating Station Equipment – RTU which 
has experienced more retirement activity and is comparable in service life and net salvage 
parameters to this account.

Table 1. Account Parameters and Statistics
 
Service Life and Net Salvage Estimation 
Only $875 of investment has been retired from this account since 1996, the earliest year in the 
observation band. The average plant balance over that period was more than $1.7M.  
Because no statistical service life analysis could be conducted and without any evidence to the 
contrary, Foster Associates is recommending a retention of the currently prescribed service life 
and net salvage parameters.

--- Page 164 ---
  
NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Distribution Plant    
Non-Leak-Prone 
Account: 375.00  Structures and Improvements 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 1 of 1 
 
Description 
This account includes the cost of structures and related facilities used in gas distribution 
operations. Costs in this account fall into categories such as sitework, paving, windows, and new 
facilities. A significant portion of the investment in this account is associated with service 
centers and administrative buildings located in various operating divisions. Account statistics and 
current and proposed parameters are shown in Table 1 below. 
 
Current
Proposed
PLife-Curve
45-L0
45-L1
Future Net Sal. Rate
-40.0%
-50.0%
Realized Net Sal. Rate
-64.6%
Average Age (yrs.)
20.6
Derived Additions
$8,214,682
Plant Retirements
$1,515,039
Percent Retired 
18.4%
Plant Balance
$6,699,643
Table 1. Account Parameters and Statistics
 
Service Life Analysis 
The statistical service life analysis for this account is based on reasonably organized retirement 
rates which provide supportable indications. Broader bands with low censoring and conformance 
indexes indicate average service lives between the low– to high–40s with lower–modal left 
dispersions. 
Life Estimation 
Based on these observations, Foster Associates is recommending retention of the currently 
prescribed 45 year projection life and a revision to an L1 projection curve. 
Net Salvage Analysis 
The adjusted net salvage analysis for this account shows an overall realized net salvage rate of -
64.6 percent and a trend to higher costs of retiring. Realized net salvage rates, for example were 
respectively -75.4 and -147.1 percent for the 10– and five–year rolling bands ending in 2018. 
Net Salvage Estimation 
Based on the net salvage analysis and the expectation of increasing negative net salvage, a -50 
percent future net salvage rate is recommended for this account to move the expectation in the 
direction of the historical indications.

NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Distribution Plant    
Non-Leak-Prone 
Account: 376.11  Mains - Steel 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 1 of 3 
 
Description 
This account includes the installed cost of steel distribution system mains, from the transmission 
line to the customer service line. Account statistics and current and proposed parameters are 
shown in Table 1 below. 
 
Current
Proposed
PLife-Curve

Table 1. Account Parameters and Statistics
 
Life Analysis 
Forces of retirement acting upon metallic mains include corrosion of the pipe to an extent that it 
is no longer cost effective to clamp or repair. An added force of retirement acting upon both 
metallic and plastic mains is replacement of older, low pressure lines with high pressure lines 
designed to accommodate growth in demand, highway relocations and widening of intersections. 
The statistical service life analysis for this account indicates service lives exceeding 100 years 
for most broader bands. The remaining bands show average service lives no shorter than 95 
years. 
Investment of $2,958,464 in leak–prone steel pipes (LPP) at December 31, 2018 was identified 
by the Company and transferred to a separate subaccount, 376.31, for proactive and accelerated 
replacement. 
Life Estimation 
Although the Company expects that the ongoing substitution of plastic for metallic mains will 
continue to drive down the average service lives observed for metallic mains, it is difficult to 
predict the magnitude of the effect on the average service life of this account. In its "Reactive 
Main Replacement Program", the Company expects to replace smaller sections of main segments 
and associated services that are identified during leak surveys and cannot be repaired by simple 
leak clamps. This program will allow Field Operations to make quick decisions on replacing 
actively deteriorating segments of pipe without Gas Engineering approval. The program covers 
the Company's inventory of (i) non–cathodically protected steel pipe (coated or bare), (ii) cast 
and wrought iron pipe, and (iii) pre–1985 Dupont Aldyl–A plastic pipe. 
Without a clear indication at this time of the extent to which these observations and initiatives 
will affect the future measured average service life of this account, Foster Associates is 
Exhibit___(KAK-3)
Page 113 of 441


--- Page 183 ---
  
NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Distribution Plant    
Non-Leak-Prone 
Account: 376.11  Mains - Steel 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 2 of 3 
 
recommending a retention of the currently prescribed 95–H4 projection life–curve for this 
account. 
Net Salvage Analysis 
The adjusted five–year moving average analysis for this account continues to show highly 
negative net salvage rates. The overall realized net salvage rate was -135.8 percent and the five–
year moving average rate was -129 percent at the end of 2018. 
Net Salvage Estimation 
No gross salvage is expected from retired pipe. Cost associated with the retirement or removal of 
pipe, however, can be substantial. Although the nature of the work needed to retire or remove 
pipe is situational, it typically involves a number of costly activities such as conditioning the site 
(i.e. obtaining permits, setting up signs and checking for the presence of other shared utilities, a 
process called "Dig Safe"), stopping the flow of gas and purging the pipe of all remaining gas, 
checking for contaminants, digging, excavating, cutting, capping, backfilling with grout, 
abandoning in place or removing pipe for safe and environmentally acceptable disposal since 
used pipe is always considered contaminated. Site restoration is invariably mandatory. 
Equipment such as backhoes, excavators, dump trucks and "suck–down trucks" which extract the 
remaining gas out of the line and into adjacent lines are almost invariably required at the site. 
Coordination with other operations personnel is often required to adjust station pressures, operate 
valves, redirect the flow of gas in order to reduce pressures and vent as little gas as possible to 
the atmosphere (for environmental reasons). All removed pipe requires testing for PCBs and the 
positive results dictate specific abandonment procedures. The cost incurred in retiring, replacing, 
or abandoning a main is, therefore, labor intensive, sensitive to inflation, and substantial. 
Some retirement and removal jobs will activate Article VII which is a New York State Code to 
demonstrate the need for a particular project and additionally provide the state a thorough study 
of need and the environmental impact of the project. Article VII is mandated depending on the 
size of the project and oftentimes takes years to complete the requisite documentation and obtain 
approval. 
The need to physically remove pipe is dictated by a number of conditions. Pipe removal is 
dictated when it is in the way of new construction, when issuance of a permit requires it, or in the 
case pipe running into or through a regulator station. Pipe retired in place near or in the ground 
of a regulator station can affect corrosion prevention and also prove problematic when 
congestion of retired pipe and equipment prevents proper identification of assets. All pipe retired 
in place must be tested for liquids and actions taken if any are found. Actions could include 
directing a foam pig through the pipe to absorb and push out the liquids in a controlled manner. 
If PCBs are found during wipe sample testing, affected sections may need to be grouted or 
excavated and removed. Pipe passing under road ways, rail road tracks or water bodies requires 
removal to prevent damage if the pipe settled or corroded over time and could no longer support 
the sustained loading. 
Exhibit___(KAK-3)
Page 114 of 441


--- Page 184 ---
  
NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Distribution Plant    
Non-Leak-Prone 
Account: 376.11  Mains - Steel 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 3 of 3 
 
One of the factors dictating physical removal of mains is the availability of rights of way. With 
increasing real estate development and highway construction, rights of way availability is 
expected to continue declining in the future. 
The development of a net salvage rate for distribution steel mains should also recognize that 
removal expense incurred in any given year is largely independent of the age of the plant retired 
from service. Moreover, because future removal expense is sensitive to inflation, it is necessary 
to consider the timing of future retirements and the age of the plant when it will most likely be 
retired from service. 
Both the historical realized net salvage and the discussion above support the expectation that 
substantial cost of retiring and removal will be incurred in the future. Based on these 
observations, Foster Associates is recommending a -60 percent future net salvage rate for this 
account to move the rate from the currently approved -40 percent and in the direction of this 
expectation.

Description 
This account includes the installed cost of plastic distribution system mains, from the 
transmission line to the customer service line. Account statistics and current and proposed 
parameters are shown in Table 1 below.

Service Life Analysis 
The Company started installing plastic pipe in the early–1970s. Although metallic pipe was the 
dominant material type in the past, plastic pipe is now the material of choice because it is lighter, 
flexible and easier to install. Plastic is also more resistant to corrosive soil conditions and costs 
less per unit to install than metallic pipe. Forces of retirement acting upon plastic mains include 
slow–crack growth, damage from frost, vibrations, faulty installations, trenching and 
excavations. The design life of plastic mains is often cited as 50 years. 
Only $7.2M of investment has been retired from this account since 1996, the earliest year in the 
observation band. The average plant balance over that period was more than $571M. The derived 
retirement rates on which the statistical service life analysis is based are insubstantial and tend to 
generate negative estimators of the hazard function. Broader observation bands are highly 
censored and do not provided reliable service life estimates. Reasonable conclusions could not, 
therefore, be drawn from the statistical service life analysis. 
Life Estimation 
In its "Reactive Main Replacement Program", the Company expects to replace smaller sections 
of main segments and associated services that are identified during leak surveys and cannot be 
repaired by simple leak clamps. This program will allow Field Operations to make quick 
decisions on replacing actively deteriorating segments of pipe without Gas Engineering approval. 
The program covers the Company's inventory of (i) non–cathodically protected steel pipe (coated 
or bare), (ii) cast and wrought iron pipe, and (iii) pre–1985 Dupont Aldyl–A plastic pipe. 
Without a clear indication at this time of the extent to which these observations and initiatives 
will affect the future measured average service life of this account, Foster Associates is 
recommending a retention of the currently prescribed 65-H4 projection life-curve for this 
account.

Net Salvage Analysis 
The adjusted net salvage analysis for this account continues to show highly negative net salvage 
rates. The overall realized net salvage rate was -73.0 percent and the 5–year moving average rate 
at the end of 2018 was -41.3 percent.  
Net Salvage Estimation 
No gross salvage is expected from retired pipe. Cost associated with the retirement or removal of 
pipe, however, can be substantial. Although the nature of the work needed to retire or remove 
pipe is situational, it typically involves a number of costly activities such as conditioning the site 
(i.e. obtaining permits, setting up signs and checking for the presence of other shared utilities, a 
process called "Dig Safe"), stopping the flow of gas and purging the pipe of all remaining gas, 
checking for contaminants, digging, excavating, cutting, capping, backfilling with grout, 
abandoning in place or removing pipe for safe and environmentally acceptable disposal since 
used pipe is always considered contaminated. Site restoration is invariably mandatory. 
Equipment such as backhoes, excavators, dump trucks and "suck–down trucks" which extract the 
remaining gas out of the line and into adjacent lines are almost invariably required at the site. 
Coordination with other operations personnel is often required to adjust station pressures, operate 
valves, redirect the flow of gas in order to reduce pressures and vent as little gas as possible to 
the atmosphere (for environmental reasons). All removed pipe requires testing for PCBs and the 
positive results dictate specific abandonment procedures. The cost incurred in retiring, replacing, 
or abandoning a main is, therefore, labor intensive, sensitive to inflation, and substantial. 
Some retirement and removal jobs will activate Article VII which is a New York State Code to 
demonstrate the need for a particular project and additionally provide the state a thorough study 
of need and the environmental impact of the project. Article VII is mandated depending on the 
size of the project and oftentimes takes years to complete the requisite documentation and obtain 
approval. 
The need to physically remove pipe is dictated by a number of conditions. Pipe removal is 
dictated when it is in the way of new construction, when issuance of a permit requires it, or in the 
case pipe running into or through a regulator station. Pipe retired in place near or in the ground 
of a regulator station can affect corrosion prevention and also prove problematic when 
congestion of retired pipe and equipment prevents proper identification of assets. All pipe retired 
in place must be tested for liquids and actions taken if any are found. Actions could include 
directing a foam pig through the pipe to absorb and push out the liquids in a controlled manner. 
If PCBs are found during wipe sample testing, affected sections may need to be grouted or 
excavated and removed. Pipe passing under road ways, rail road tracks or water bodies requires 
removal to prevent damage if the pipe settled or corroded over time and could no longer support 
the sustained loading. 
One of the factors dictating physical removal of mains is the availability of rights of way. With 
increasing real estate development and highway construction, rights of way availability is 
expected to continue declining in the future.

Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 3 of 3 
 
The development of a net salvage rate for distribution plastic mains should also recognize that 
removal expense incurred in any given year is largely independent of the age of the plant retired 
from service. Moreover, because future removal expense is sensitive to inflation, it is necessary 
to consider the timing of future retirements and the age of the plant when it will most likely be 
retired from service. 
Both the historical realized net salvage and the discussion above support the expectation that 
substantial cost of retiring and removal will be incurred in the future. Based on these 
observations, Foster Associates is recommending a revision from the currently approved -30 
percent to a -40 percent future net salvage rate for this account.

NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS

Account: 376.13  Mains - Cast Iron 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 1 of 3 
 
Description 
This account includes the installed cost of cast iron distribution system mains, from the 
transmission line to the customer service line. Account statistics and current and proposed 
parameters are shown in Table 1 below.

Table 1. Account Parameters and Statistics
 
Service Life Analysis 
Cast iron is the oldest material used for gas transportation in the country. The investment in cast 
iron pipe has declined from $11.3M in 1996 to under $6.0M at the end of 2018. Less than 
$250,000 of investment has been installed over the last 10 years. 
The actuarial service life analysis for this account indicates average service lives between 60 and 
70 years for broader bands with lower censoring and conformance indexes. More recent 
narrower bands indicate average service lives as short as 25 years. 
Investment of $5,474,445 in leak–prone cast iron pipes at December 31, 2018 was identified by 
the Company and transferred to a separate subaccount, 376.33, for proactive and accelerated 
replacement. 
Life Estimation 
In its "Reactive Main Replacement Program", the Company expects to replace smaller sections 
of main segments and associated services that are identified during leak surveys and cannot be 
repaired by simple leak clamps. This program will allow Field Operations to make quick 
decisions on replacing actively deteriorating segments of pipe without Gas Engineering approval. 
The program covers the Company's inventory of (i) non–cathodically protected steel pipe (coated 
or bare), (ii) cast and wrought iron pipe, and (iii) pre–1985 Dupont Aldyl–A plastic pipe. 
Without a clear indication at this time of the extent to which these observations and initiatives 
will affect the future measured average service life of this account, Foster Associates is basing its 
estimation on the indications from the statistical service life analysis which generally support a 
shorter projection life than currently prescribed. Based on these observations, a 65–L1 projection 
life–curve is recommended for this account.

Net Salvage Analysis 
The adjusted net salvage analysis for this account continues to show highly negative net salvage 
which is reflective of the advanced age of retirements in this account. Retirements reported for 
the full band had an average age at retirement of 73.8 years. The full band realized net savage 
rate was -225.6 percent and the five–year rolling band analysis rate was -195 percent at the end 
of 2018. 
Net Salvage Estimation 
No gross salvage is expected from retired pipe. Cost associated with the retirement or removal of 
pipe, however, can be substantial. Although the nature of the work needed to retire or remove 
pipe is situational, it typically involves a number of costly activities such as conditioning the site 
(i.e. obtaining permits, setting up signs and checking for the presence of other shared utilities, a 
process called "Dig Safe"), stopping the flow of gas and purging the pipe of all remaining gas, 
checking for contaminants, digging, excavating, cutting, capping, backfilling with grout, 
abandoning in place or removing pipe for safe and environmentally acceptable disposal since 
used pipe is always considered contaminated. Site restoration is invariably mandatory. 
Equipment such as backhoes, excavators, dump trucks and "suck–down trucks" which extract the 
remaining gas out of the line and into adjacent lines are almost invariably required at the site. 
Coordination with other operations personnel is often required to adjust station pressures, operate 
valves, redirect the flow of gas in order to reduce pressures and vent as little gas as possible to 
the atmosphere (for environmental reasons). All removed pipe requires testing for PCBs and the 
positive results dictate specific abandonment procedures. The cost incurred in retiring, replacing, 
or abandoning a main is, therefore, labor intensive, sensitive to inflation, and substantial. 
Some retirement and removal jobs will activate Article VII which is a New York State Code to 
demonstrate the need for a particular project and additionally provide the state a thorough study 
of need and the environmental impact of the project. Article VII is mandated depending on the 
size of the project and oftentimes takes years to complete the requisite documentation and obtain 
approval. 
The need to physically remove pipe is dictated by a number of conditions. Pipe removal is 
dictated when it is in the way of new construction, when issuance of a permit requires it, or in the 
case pipe running into or through a regulator station. Pipe retired in place near or in the ground 
of a regulator station can affect corrosion prevention and also prove problematic when 
congestion of retired pipe and equipment prevents proper identification of assets. All pipe retired 
in place must be tested for liquids and actions taken if any are found. Actions could include 
sending foam pig through the pipe to absorb and push out the liquids in a controlled manner. If 
PCBs are found during wipe sample testing, affected sections may need to be grouted or 
excavated and removed. Pipe passing under road ways, rail road tracks or water bodies requires 
removal to prevent damage if the pipe settled or corroded over time and could no longer support 
the sustained loading. 
Exhibit___(KAK-3)
Page 154 of 441


--- Page 224 ---
  
NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Distribution Plant    
Non-Leak-Prone 
Account: 376.13  Mains - Cast Iron 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 3 of 3 
 
One of the factors dictating physical removal of mains is the availability of rights of way. With 
increasing real estate development and highway construction, rights of way availability is 
expected to continue declining in the future.  
The development of a net salvage rate for distribution cast iron mains should also recognize that 
removal expense incurred in any given year is largely independent of the age of the plant retired 
from service. Moreover, because future removal expense is sensitive to inflation, it is necessary 
to consider the timing of future retirements and the age of the plant when it will most likely be 
retired from service. 
Both the historical realized net salvage and the discussion above support the expectation that 
substantial cost of retiring and removal will be incurred in the future. Based on these 
observations, Foster Associates is recommending a retention of the currently approved -200 
percent future net salvage rate.

Table 1. Account Parameters and Statistics
 
Service Life and Net Salvage Analysis and Estimation 
Only $50,688 of investment has been retired from this account since 1996, the earliest year in the 
observation band. The average plant balance over that period was more than $9M. The derived 
retirement rates on which the statistical service life analysis is based are insubstantial and tend to 
generate negative estimators of the hazard function. Broader observation bands are highly 
censored and do not provided reliable service life estimates. Reasonable conclusions could not, 
therefore, be drawn from the statistical service life analysis.  
The adjusted net salvage analysis for this account continues to show highly negative net salvage 
rates. The overall realized net salvage rate was -403.8 percent by the end of 2018 and the five–
year moving average rate exceeded -1100 percent but was based on the minimal retirement 
activity discussed above. 
The Company requested Foster Associates to create an association between this account and 
Account 376.12 - Mains – Plastic for depreciation purposes. The parameters recommended for 
Account 376.12 are, therefore, analogously recommended for this account.

NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Distribution Plant    
Non-Leak-Prone 
Account: 378.10  Meas. and Reg. Sta. Equipment 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 1 of 2 
 
Description 
This account includes the cost of meters, filters, gauges, piping, valves, and other equipment 
used in measuring or regulating gas in connection with gas distribution operations. Account 
statistics and current and proposed parameters are shown in Table 1 below. 
 
Current
Proposed
PLife-Curve
36-L0.5

Table 1. Account Parameters and Statistics
 
Service Life Analysis 
Major forces of retirement acting on this account include corrosion, structural settlement, and 
obsolescence. Much of the piping in stations is located in damp and wet areas. Salted road runoff 
in winter corrodes vaults, piping, and metallic structures. The statistical service life analysis for 
this account is based on reasonably organized retirement rates which provide supportable 
indications. Many broader bands with lower censoring and unaffected by negative hazard rates 
show average service lives between 30 and 40 years.  
Life Estimation 
Company operations personnel report that many oil seals classified in this account are at least 40 
years old and becoming inoperable. The Company is reviewing plans to retire the old seals and 
replace them with relief valves. They also report concerns with pipe corrosion, settling of 
buildings and vaults, and antiquated technology. The Company continues to conduct either 
partial or complete station rebuilds depending upon station condition. Partial rebuilds are 
performed to upgrade stations to current technologies and replace old components. Completed 
rebuilds are performed when the station is too old to be refurbished and requires a totality of new 
buildings, piping, valves and regulators. System automation is another major undertaking to 
permit visibility to Gas System Operation and Control (GSO) so that system pressures can be 
monitored and alarms activated when pressures deviate from prescribed ranges. Beyond 
visibility, automation additionally permits GSO to operate equipment remotely using special 
motors. As technology continues to advance, the Company is expecting to use special 
temperature modules that will permit pressure control automatically based on ambient 
temperature. 
It is not known, however, to what extent the combination of these initiatives will affect the 
expected average service life of this account. Based on these observations and given the 
variability in the statistical service life analysis Foster Associates is recommending a retention of

the currently approved 36–L0.5 projection life–curve. Although the projection life parameter 
could potentially be extended to 40 year based primarily on the statistical service life analysis, 
the Company's concerns over obsolete and antiquated equipment, unaddressed pipe corrosion, 
building settlement, and expected rebuilds suggest restraint in extending the projection service 
life of this account at this time. 
Net Salvage Analysis 
The adjusted net salvage analysis for this account shows an overall realized net salvage rate of -
31.9 percent. Realized net salvage rates additionally show a mitigated trend to higher negative 
net salvage rates over the study period. The 10–year and 5–year rolling bands ending in 2018, for 
example, show realized net salvage rates of -28.2 and -38.6 respectively. 
Net Salvage Estimation 
Although it is possible to salvage equipment such as valves, regulators, chart boxes, and system 
automation device depending on condition, most materials are considered contaminated and 
disposed of using mandated and environmentally acceptable procedures. Cost of removal in this 
account is generated from a number of events such as conditioning the site for the start of a job 
(e.g. setting up signs, mobilizing, and "Dig Safe" checking), digging and excavating, welding, 
cutting and capping, rental costs associated with equipment such as backhoes, excavators, and 
dump trucks, stopping gas flow, and purging the gas to clear lines. Additional retiring and 
removal costs are due to cutting and removing the old assets, backfilling holes, and site 
restoration.  Based on the net salvage analysis, the currently prescribed -30 percent future net 
salvage rate continues to be a reasonable estimation and is, therefore, retained for this account.

NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Distribution Plant    
Non-Leak-Prone 
Account: 378.20  Meas. and Reg. Sta. Equip. - Manholes 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 1 of 1 
 
Description 
This account includes the installed cost of manholes and vaults used in connection with 
distribution measuring or regulating operations. Account statistics and current and proposed 
parameters are shown in Table 1 below. 
 
Current
Proposed
PLife-Curve
45-L0
45-L1
Future Net Sal. Rate
-35.0%
-40.0%
Realized Net Sal. Rate
-47.4%
Average Age (yrs.)
25.3
Derived Additions
$1,732,901
Plant Retirements
$444,107
Percent Retired 
25.6%
Plant Balance
$1,288,794
Table 1. Account Parameters and Statistics
 
Service Life Analysis 
The actuarial service life analysis for this account indicates average service lives between 40 and 
50 years for broader bands with lower censoring and conformance indexes. 
Life Estimation 
Based on the statistical service life analysis, Foster Associates is recommending a retention of 
the currently prescribed 40–year projection life and a revision to an L1 projection life curve. 
Net Salvage Analysis 
The adjusted net salvage analysis for this account continues to show highly negative net salvage 
rates. The overall realized net salvage rate was -47.4 percent by the end of 2018 and the five–
year moving average rate was -42.9 percent.  
Net Salvage Estimation 
Based on the historical net salvage analysis, a -40 percent future net salvage rate is recommended 
for this account.

378.55 Meas. and Reg. Station Equipment - RTU 
 
 
378.55 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Exhibit___(KAK-3)

--- Page 295 ---
  
NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Distribution Plant    
Non-Leak-Prone 
Account: 378.55  Meas. and Reg. Station Equip. - RTU 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 1 of 1 
 
Description 
This account includes the cost of remote terminal units (RTUs) used to provide remote control 
and information acquisition such as temperature, pressure, and flow data between distribution 
gas regulating substations and manned Control Centers. Where required, RTUs can also monitor 
gas detectors and intrusion alarms and allow Control Centers to adjust flow and pressure set 
points at regulator stations. Data is transmitted via phone lines or cellular networks. Account 
statistics and current and proposed parameters are shown in Table 1 below.

Table 1. Account Parameters and Statistics
 
Service Life Analysis 
The derived retirement rates on which the statistical service life analysis is based are 
insubstantial and tend to generate negative estimators of the hazard function. Broader 
observation bands are highly censored and do not provided reliable service life estimates. 
Reasonable conclusions could not, therefore, be drawn from the statistical service life analysis.  
Life Estimation 
Some of the equipment such as modems and telemetry was installed many years ago and has 
become obsolete or antiquated. About 44 percent of the Company's system, for example, 
continues to rely on paper chart recorders instead of electronic system automation. The 
Company, through its "System Automation & Control" program, intends to update the equipment 
to support the standardization of telemetry across the Company's gas transmission and 
distribution system. 
Based on these observations and without any evidence to the contrary, Foster Associates is 
recommending a retention of the currently prescribed service life parameters for this account. 
Net Salvage Analysis 
The adjusted net salvage analysis shows a -9.3 percent overall realized net salvage rate and a -
63.1 percent five-year rolling average at the end of 2018. The 2018 five–year average, however, 
is based on minimal retirements over that period. 
Net Salvage Estimation 
Based on these observations, Foster Associates is recommending a retention of the currently 
prescribed -5 percent future net salvage rate.

NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION - GAS  
Distribution Plant    
Non-Leak-Prone 
Account: 380.10  Services - Metallic 
 
Account Analysis and Parameter Estimation 
        
 
Page 1 of 3 
 
Description 
This account includes the installed cost of metallic distribution services. A complete service 
begins with the connection on the main and extends to but does not include the connection with 
the customer's meter.  A stub service extends from the main to the property line, or the curb stop. 
Services which have been used but have become inactive are retired from utility plant in service 
immediately if there is no prospect for reuse, and, in any event, are retired by the end of the 
second year following that during which the service became inactive unless reused in the interim. 
Account statistics and current and proposed parameters are shown in Table 1 below.
